DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Gentle.en:
Attachol hereto is an application for per t to enter
occupicd territories in the case of Antonios D. Karalis!
Mr. Karalis was employed as a Greck seaman until he was
capturcdby a German raider in the North Atlantic in March, 1942. He was
taken to Japan wherc he was interned until V-J Day. After V-J Day he was
employed as a seaman for the Army Transport Service. He was discharged
from ATS because of a law prohibiting non-citizens serving in this capacity.
He was then employed by the Yokohama Plant of the Pepsi-Cola Company which
is bottling Pepsi-Cola for the United States Armed Forces. His services
were very satisfactory and it is the desire of this Company to hire him in
the same capacity. His reason for leaving our employ was to visit the United
States in connection with indemmities and back pay owed him by the Imbiricos
Shimping Company. He is married and his wife is living in Japan.
Since we do not know how lonf it will be before he will be
required to leave the United States, we should appreciate your expediting
this rermit so thet when Mr. Karalis must leave the country he can return to
Janan and to our employ.
With thanks for the courtesies you have extended us, we remmn
Yours very truly,
PFPSI-COLA GOMPANY

FOR COMPASSIONATE REASONS
I CERTIFY THAT I HAVE READ AD UNDERSTAND THE FOLLOWING:
A. AS A VISITOR TO GERMANY I MUST PROVIDE MY OWN FACILITIES WHICH WILL REQUIRE ME TO MAINTAIN TENPORA
RY RESIDENCE WITH MY RELATIVE FOR THE DURATION OF MY STAY.
B. THAT UNLESS I WANT TO GO HUNGRY. I SHOULD SHIP OR BRING WITH ME. SUFFICIENT FOOD FOR NY ON USE TO
AUGUMENT THE 1SOO CALORIES PER DAY AUTHORIZED FOR PURCHASE WITH GERMAN RATION COUPONS.
C. IF DESIRED. I MUST TAKE SUCH EVERY-DAY COMMODITIES AS SOAP, TOILET ARTICLES. TOBACCO. MEDICINE. ETC.
WITH ME AS SUCH ITEMS CANMOT BE PURCHASED IN GERMNY.
D. THAT COAL OR ANY TYPE OF FUEL IS EXTREMELY SCARCE AND DURING THE WINTER MONTNS I SHOULD GO TO GER-
MANY WITH THE EXPECTATION OF BEING COLD AT ALL TIMES AND SHOULD TAKE CLOTHING SUITABLE TO ALLEVIATE SUCN COMDI-
TIONS.
E. THAT ALL GERMAN TRANSPORT FACILITIES ARE EXTREMELY OVERBURDENED AND THAT I WILL NOT E ALLOED TO
USE THE FEW AMERICAN TRAINS WHICH ARE BEING OPERATED.
F. NO MEANS EXIST OR WILL BE MADE AVAILABLE FOR CHANGING ANERICAN DOLLARS TO MILITARY PAYNENT CERTIFI-
FINANCE OFFICES FOR GERMAN REICHMARKS. BUT ANY REICHMARKS REMAINING AT THE END OF THE TRIP WILE MOT BE ACCEPTED
IN EXCHANGE FOR DOLLARS.
G. THE MAXIMUM LENGTH OF TIME I CAN REMAIN IN GERMANY IS 3O DAYS AND UNDER NO CINCUMBTANCES WILL NV
VISIT BE EXTENDED BEYOND THIS PERIOD. RETURN TRANSPORTATION HAS BEEN BOOKED TO TANE ME OUT OF GERNNY WIININ TNE
PRESCRIBED TIME LIMIT.
I FURTHER CERTIFY THAT ALL INFORMATION GIVEN IN THIS APPLICATION IS TRUE TO TME BEST OF NY MOLEDE AND BE-

LLPARNMENT OF STATE
Mr. O'Brien called Miss Flickinger to clarify a memorandum dated
December 5 from Colonel Scott of the Military Permit Office to Mrs.
Shipley. This memo states that Mr. Clark made local application in
Japan to re-enter Japan as a representative of a Chicago firm, and
that the Military Fermit Section informed SCAP that it has no ob-
jection to Mr. Clark's re-entry.
Miss Flickinger stated that SCAp has cleared Mr. Clark for the
usual twenty-one day period. Mr. Clark's entry is in accordance with
the appropriate paragraphs of SCAP Circular 8 of August 30, 1946.
Miss Flickinger also stated that she understands SCAp will communi-
cate with Mr. Clark, but,at Mr. O'Erien's suggestion,it hinks State
should also communicate regarding this case since State had handled
CS
the documentation of Mr. Clark previously.

TO WHOM IT MAY CONCERN
I. Moses E. Kaptzan was born 1n Nikolaevak, Siberia on July 25th. 19ll.
I lived in Nikolaevsk until I mms 8 yeare of age, and in l919 after
my father was brutally killed by the bolshevik partisans, I left for
Japan in the company of my mother, my brother and three sisters.
I arrived 1n Japan (Hakodate) 1n June l9lg and after staying for a few
months with my uncle Mr. M.M. Lury. we all moved to Yokohama where I
entered the St. Joseph' College, and began my studies in the English
language.
I lived in Yokohama until 1923. when after the earthquake of September
lst. 1923, I moved to Harbin in the company of my mother, my brother and
my three sisters.
I lived in Harbin for nine months, where I picked up and improved my
Russian language, and in the autumn of 1924 we all moved to China.
I arrived in Tientsin in 1924 and entered the Tientain Grammar School,
from where I graduated in 19.8.
Right after I finished school I took a job with the China Fur Trading
Co.. Ltd. and worked 1n this firm in Tientsin until 1933.
In 1933 I was transferred to Dairen, and was placed in charge of our
branch office there. My functione were selling import goods, and also
since we were buying furs for ernort, I used to travel frequently to
Japan and Manchuria. I lived in Dairen until 1939, when I decided to
resign from my position and moved to Shanghai.
In 1935 I married Miss. Irene Lewinson in Tientsin, where I came on
a mhort trip.
In 1939 I arrived in Shanghai accompanied by my wife, and in September
of that year our son Anthony was born.
Arriving 1n Shangha1 in May 1939 I was offered and took the position of
manager of the firm of Leon S. Weinstein, and was engaged in buying furs
for shipment to the United States of America, as well as selling imported
furs in China. I worked for this firm until the outbreak of war in the
Pacific, and being torn away from communication with the United States
I began the liquidation of this company.
During the war years I lived in Shanghai, at first working on my own, and
in 1g43 formed a partnership with Alex Shvetz. We worked together for two
years and 1n the middle of 1945 terminated our partnership. With the end
of hostilities I formed a company in Shanghai under the title of Hma Me1
Trading Co., and in 1gl6 after arriving in the United States I formed another
corporation under the name of Hwa Mei Trading Corp., in New York.
In February 1gu6 I applied for a business visa to the United States, and
having received such, left Shanghai on a short trip in March 1946.
I arrived 1n New York on May 2nd., 19ht accompanied by my wife, who came with
me to undergo a very delicate eye ove tion, and she roturned to Shanghai
in September 1946.
I remained in the United States until December 28th., when my wife returned
accompanied by our Bon, and we left Ner York for Mexico on December 28th.
1glht. My stay in the United States was good intil Merch 26th. 1946.
I am now living in Mexico. havine arrived in tnie country as an imeigrant,
and my family is with me ander the same status.
Since landing in Japan in 1919 and unti1 February 1at, I have alwaye lived
in the Orient as a stateless, and only in February 19it, ix conmliance with
a decree 1seued by the USSR government of January 20th. 19c, I have applied
for Russian citizenship. I recelved a temporary certificate issued by the
USSR consulate in Shanghai, iated Fetra ry 2oth. 1att, and although thi
certificete hed to oe changed for a regular passport after six months of
1ts issuance. I have not dont s as yet Ail tht time. I have been asking

=== Page 21 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2-
and getting short extensions of my temporary certificate.
At the moment being an immigrant to Mexico, and a resident of this country
I have applied for a travelling passport from the Foreign Office of the
Medcan Government, and have been promised this document where I will be
listed as stateless and a resident of Mexico.
In all probability I will have this document wthin a week from today, and
upon receiving such will forward additional information regarding this
passport.
As of September 3rd. 1946 my application for entering the United States of
America has been registered with the American Consulate in Mexico City, and
from information received. I understand that all my papere are in order, and
now I am waiting my turn on the quota waiting list to come, which will
enable me and my family to move to the United States of America, where I
hope to make my permanent home.
Moses E. Kaptzan
True Copy.
James Michelson

Government.

suBJECT: Travel of alien m1ssionaries to Japan.
With reference to Mr. Chappell's question conoerning
Colonel Soott' proposal to change sub-paragraph 2 of
paragraph 7 of the attached SCAp memorandum, 1t 1s believed
that the proposed change will not affect the work of this
Division which consists, in this instance, of the delivery
of Military Permits to alien missionaries because of the
following reasons:
1.
Aliens are still bound by the provisions of
Circular 8 requiring that their cases be
894
presented to SCAP by representatives of their
own.government in Japan or through proper
diplomatic channels. In practice, in the
case of missionaries this has been amended so
that their cases have been presented to SCAP
by representatives of their religious organ-
1zatione who are in Japan.
2.
Our experlence does not reveal a single case
2-
where an alien misslonary's case has been
presented directly to this Division. We come
2
2347
into the picture only after clearance has been
secured.
It 1s thought, however, that SCAP's reference in its
pronosed communication to the Mission Boards in Japan (see
paragraph 7) 1s weak and that some where it should be
spelled out clearly that the liberalized policy does not
do away with Circular 8 in the cases of al1ens.
e
PD may or may not object to receiving initially American
cases but that 1a the problem for them to decide.

January 13, 1948
Mr. Frank J. .olan
United States Department of Commerce
350 P1fth Avenue
New York 1, New York
Dear Mr. Nolan:
Hr. A. N. Petersen, Nanager of our correspondent Far Bast
uperinte: dence :o. Ltd., Kobe, writes that he should go
to lokio from time to time to discuss business with the
CAP and otner people located tuere, but the drawback 1s
tne lack of hotel accommodations at Tokio.
Hr. Petersen understands that a number of local foreign
traders (residents) have been appointed representatives
of 'nited States Commercial nouses, and these people can
make use of the few hotels available to foreign traders
by paying in nited States currency, respectively United
States Traders Certificates.
Mr. Petersen asks us to examine this question and if possi-
ble arrange with the United States Department of Commerce
to have him appointed our personal representative in Japan.
In case this can be arranged Mr. Petersen asks us to write
confirming the appointment - original and five copies -
approved by the United States Department of Commerce. He
can then have his name registered with the Foreign Trade
Division of SCAP and the Boeke-Cho (Japanese Board of Trade)
and obtain permit to use the hotel facilities available, and
he will also be able to obtain from the National City Bank
of New York, Tokio, the necessary Traders Certificates to pay
for hotel accommodations out of dollar remittances made to
CS/V
him.
You are familiar with the inspection and supervision of mer-
chandise we are making here, and we expect that we will be
called upon to carry out through our correspondents similar
work in Japan. We have already received inquires about Crab
Meat, Frozen Sword Fish, Scallops, Frog Legs.
If we get such work it will be necessary for Mr. Petersen to
be able to travel about as he will have to go from Kobe to carry
out the inspections, and we shall be obliged if you will advise
how we should go about to obtain the necessary permit.
Very truly yours,
SUPERINTEN
B
Secretary

=== Page 86 === (confidence 0.95)
SUPERINTENDENCE OF SHIPMENTS
Shipments of wheat abroad are inv ieed to the buyer on official
seaboard weights as shown in the occan till of lading. The American
prain contract of the London Corn Trade Association, which expresses
the terms upon which practically all -ales of our wheat are made
abroad, contains the provision that deficiencies in bill-of-lading
weights -hall be paid tor by the buver. Consequently, the seller
guatantres the outturn. In shipping to a few English points, notably
London and Hull. and almost uniformly in shipping to the Continent,
the cu-tom i- to sell "full outtuin guar anteed. but in sades to most
I mited kmgdom pont- the guaranty covets outtum within 1 per
nt.**
The i-k in ured by guatantecing outtun is considerable, for at
many forcign port- the facihtie- fot unloading and weighing are poor
and the methods of handling ate cateless Also, prompt settlements
of weight differences with far away buvers are difficult, and dis
pute- frequently arise. In order to avoid the multitude of detail in
looking after deliveries and effecting settlements, and also to reduce
to a determinable figure the cost of the responsibility assumed by
guar anteeing delivered weights, most exporters avail themselves of
the servi es of an organization formed to supervise the unloading and
weighing of shipments at foreign ports and to make adjustments of
all difliculties direct with the buver. The contracts which exporters
enter into with the organization transfers the responsibility for dif-
ferences in outturn and the settlement of such differences from the
expotter to the organization.
The primary functions of a superintendence company, as described
by the manager of a large fim -pecializing in such business, are
1, to supervise deliveties and bring pressure to bear to improve
method- of -hipments. di-chatge, and weighing: 12, to guarantee out-
turm. Such a company strives to reduce the element of negligence in
shipping and to effect prompt and just settlement of differences in
weights. The tates charged for service and other details of the con-
tract- made with clients are shown in the following agreement:
grrement printed in original climinated in this reprint
One large company in the United States carries on its activities
mmaundy through an arrangement with the So lete Generale de Sur-
veillance. S A, Geneva. Switzetland. which maintans a large staff
of traumed cmpbosres and cover- pras tieally crety port of importance
in Furope.

A valuable service rendered by a superintendence company is the
fur nishing of up-to date information on port conditions and foreign
redits The credt nfermation furnished covers buyers and pros-
pe tive buscts, eporters agents, and persons seeking ageney posi-
tons. The information is often of great assistance in avoiding losses.
The intimate charactet of the credit reports is aptly indicated by the
followme quotation from one of them:
Elimmated m thus repint
In stew of the fat tlung trade of the grain exporter, it is natural
that mans desagreements should arise ovet the settlement of weight
differeue es. and when these ate brought to arbitration the superin-
temdencee company is often helpful in handling the cases for the
shuppers and appointing arbitrators on their behalf. The selection
of a foreign arbitrator is rather a delicate matter, as a man who
may be good in this position for one case may not be quite as good
a- somebody clse fot another case.
Somethimg has been accomplished through superintendence work
toward overe oming carelessness and inefhcieney in port practices
and in a large measure, port conditions have ceased to concern the
expotter because of the responsibility assumed by the superinten-
dence otganization.
Thes have been of service also in other ways. For example, it
has been customary for some shippers to book ocean freights and
send grain forwatd unsold in expectation of finding a buyer upon its
arrival. In such ca-es it has sometimes been necessary for the shipper
to discharge and stote the grain, and the superintendence company,
through its tepresentative at the port of discharge. has been of
serviee in the emergeney. Also, it has net been uncommon for a
rertain class of foreign buvers, especially with a dropping market, to
try to delay pay ment of documents through settling up some alleged
grievance In such cases the superintendence company has often

EMORANDUH FOR THE FILES
Cn March 19, 1948 a lady called Mr. Corkran 1n D8
and 1dentified herself as a friend of the Ambassador of
Argentina and also as a friend of Mr. Americo Guerra, for
whom Ds has made application for permiseion to enter Japan
at the request of the Emtassy of Argentina. The lady
asked what progress was being made on the application,
and Mr. Corkran informed her that the Department had just
received a memorandum from the Military Permit Section
tating that more information will be needed regarding
the purpose of Nr. Guerra's proposed trip to Japan.
The lady called again today and aaid that she was
sending a cablegram to Mr. Guerra and asked just what
sort of additional information might prove helpful. Mr.
Corkran said that if Mr. Guerra could identify himeelf
as being associated with a business firm which did busi-
ness with Japan before the war his application probably
would be more favorably considered. The lady said that
T
Mr. Guerra 1s in fact associated with such a business
firn and she would suggest to him that he put the fact
into his amended application.

: DS - Mr. Williams
DATE: February 25, 1948
FROM : OE - Mr. Whitman
SUBJECT: Procedure for Obtaining SCAP Clearanca to Enter Japan.
SCAP replied on February 9 in his 2 38191 (State control number 4847)
to our proposal that countries be permitted to seek permission for their
nationals to enter Japan by estaulishing contact with SCAP through any
mission accredited to SCAP and not necessarily through U.S. diplomatic.
channeb. SCAP's reply states that the language of paragraph 7 a of SCAP
Circular No. 8 of 1946 was meant to imply that this was permissible
00
providing a country not having representation in Japan approached SCAP
6
through the Tokyo mission of the Governnent with which it had a formal
"protecting power" agreement.
Attaolhed is the SCAP list of "protecting power" agreements as of
November 24. It is suggested that your division prepare a circular note
3e Returned
or series of notes to the Washington Embassies of Governments named in
this list calling their attention to the substance of SCAP's Z38191.
2
It is our opinion that the burden of clearing non-US nationals for
entry into Japan from countries other than those having missions in Japa)
is likely to increase and that it would for this reason if for no other Ul
be desirable to relieve U.S. diplomatic and Army channels of communicatins
of this burdensome bit of routine.
8
The attached reference list should be returned to DRF.
R Wth the
Attachment:
Refarance List of the Allied
Council for Japan, etc.

March 15, 1948.
MEMORANDUM FOR THE FILES
Following the receipt of the underlying note of
March 10, 1948 from the Embassy of Paraguay concerning
the desire of Hr. Juan A. de la Cruz, the Consul General
of Paraguay at New York, to visit Japan, Mr. de la Cruz
v1s1ted Ds in person to give the information necessary
for the completion of the regular application forme used
in connection with travel to Japan.
Mr. de la Cruz said that he intends to leave for the
Far East within a week. Upon being informed that a
military permit to enter Japan probably could not be
obtained for him before he leaves, Mr. de la Cruz asked
1f there 1s not some way that the permit could be sent to
him, possibly while he is in China. Mr. Corkran informed
Mr. de la Cruz that notification of his Theater clearance
could be sent by cable to an American Foreign Bervice
post near where he might be at the time. Mr. de la Cruz
said that upon his arrival in China he will send a cable-
gram to the Division of Protective Services informing it
of where he will be in China at various times. Mr. Corkran
u). 8
then said that when Theater clearance 1s received for him
a cablegram will be sent to the American consular office
nearest to where his schedule calls for him to be at the
time. Mr. de la Cruz said that the bill for the cable-
F
gram may be sent to the Paraguayan Consulate General in
New York.
三

AH

Arter the telephone conversation with Miss Delaney of
the Cosa Corooratlon, of which the underlying memorandum 1s
a summary, Mr. Corkran visited the Exit Permit Unit of the
Visa Division and discussed with Mr. Hutchison and Mr.
Spence the whole question of exit permits and waivers for
aliens departing from the United States for Japan or Germany.
Mr. Hutchison and Mr. Spence sa1d that in all cases
the Exit Permit Unit will automatically grant a waiver to
-1848
any alien leaving for occunied areas for whom it 1s request-
ed by Ds. Granting a waiver conslsts of calling Miss
Schottofer of the Imm1gratlon and Naturalization Service in
Philadelphia who in turn notifies the departure control
officer at the designated port of departure.
With regard to Mr. Mueller of the Cosa Corporation,
therefore, Mr. Spence suggested that DS not send him
Forms AD-l, but rather we should write to Miss Delaney and
tell her we will arrange for a waiver in Mr. Mueller's
case 1f they will inform us of hie date and plnce of
denarture from the United States.

HC/ik
D.1
March 31, 1948.
MEMORANDUM FOR THE FILES
F
Mr. Willlams talked today with Captain Rawle of the
Military Permit Section, Civil Affalrs Div1sion, Army
Department, concerning the request of March 23, 1948 from
89
the Egyptian Embassy that application be made through
American channels for permission to travel to Japan for
Mr. Isaac Cohen, an Egyptian national. In this connection
the case of Mr. Joseph Cassuto, also an Egyptian wanting
to travel to Japan, was recalled. In the Cassuto case,
the Department agreed to handle the application only as a
result of the most urgent insistence by the Embassy and
after a formal note had actually been despatched to the
33
Embassy saying that in as much as Egyptian interests are
23
protected in Japan by Great Britain the application of
Cassuto should be forwarded through British channels.
48
Captain Rawls repeated his previously-stated view
that American communication facilities are already over-
burdened and that there 1s no reason whatever why the
State and Army Departments should concern themselves with
Egyptian applications for travel to Japan when Egyptian
interests in Japan are protected by the British.
Mr. Williame pointed out that the door could not be
C
closed entirely on the application of Egyptians,or
S
persons of any other nationalwhose intereste are pro-
tected in Japan by- third Power because first, the pro-
A
visions of the original Circular 8 have not yet been
withdrawn or formally modified 1n the respect of such
persons and, second, because it 1e the duty both of the
Army Department and the Department of State to asslst
SCAP 1n the smooth operation of the occupation and that
thie may mean in exceptional cases that such applications
should be hendled through Washington. Mr. Williame etated
his underetanding of the administrative probleme of the
Military Fermit Section and eaid that the Department would
make every effort to discourage the Egyptiane from trying
to have the permit work processed through Washington in
the abeence of very special circumetances.
Because

=== Page 476 === (confidence 0.97)
DOIR
- 2-
Cohen's
Because Mr./smmsmtmts application had been submitted
before there had been any chance for the news of ohanged
procedures to reach Cairo, and in order to avoid needless
delay in the handling of the applioation, however, Captain
Rawls agreed to accept it for transmission to the Supreme
Commander for consideration. But this action on the part
of the Military Permit Section was taken with the under-
standing that the Department would make such requeste in
the future on behalr of Egypt or of any other country
whose interests are protected in Japan by a third Power
only in the most unusual and urgent of circumstanoes.
Mr. Williame said that the Egyptian Embassy in Washington
and the American Embassy at Cairo would be informed
accordingly.
